)

The reduced pair
(W_{\mathrm{red}},\,b_{\mathrm{red}})
plugs into

brickwork_rtn_average
with the same interface as the raw
objects; the only change the reader observes is a smaller bond
dimension and a correspondingly faster contraction.

At the level of representation theory,
the procedure is an instance of Schur–Weyl duality on a finite
representation: the commutant of
\mathrm{U}(d)
acting diagonally on

k
tensor factors is spanned by
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{S}_{k}]
, but on a
representation of finite dimension this span is typically proper. For
qubits, the span is exactly the Temperley–Lieb algebra

\mathsf{TL}_{k}(\delta=2)

[
51
]
; the
positive-eigenvalue subspace of
\mathsf{Id}=G^{+}G
is

\mathsf{TL}_{k}
itself, and its dimension is the Catalan number
C_{k}
.
For qutrits one is building,
mutatis mutandis
, the analogous
quotient of
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{S}_{k}]
with respect to the kernel of the
restriction map to
\mathrm{End}((\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\otimes k})
.

3
Noisy random circuits

3.1
Noise models

This section studies how noise affects the dynamics of information in
random quantum circuits. We start with the simplest case of an
identical depolarising channel acting on every site after every
layer, and then turn to the asymmetric situation in which only one
copy of the circuit is hit by noise while the other is the ideal
quantum-state output, the natural setting for benchmarking
quantum-classical correlations
[
1
,
2
,
50
]
.

We focus on the local single-qudit
depolarising channel
, which
on a
d
-dimensional qudit reads

\mathcal{N}_{p}(\rho)\;=\;(1-p)\,\rho\;+\;p\,\frac{\mathbb{I}_{d}}{d}\,\mathrm{tr}(\rho),\qquad p\in[0,1],

(58)

and the noisy circuit applies the tensor product

\mathcal{N}_{p}^{\otimes N}
to the global state after every brickwork
layer. Concretely, every site is independently replaced by the
local maximally-mixed state with probability
p
, and left untouched
with probability
1-p
. At
p=0
this is the identity (clean
evolution); at
p=1
each layer resets the chain to the maximally
mixed state
\mathbb{I}_{d}/d
on every site. In the doubled-space
representation
\mathcal{N}_{p}
has the Choi matrix

\mathcal{N}_{p}\;=\;(1-p)\,\mathbb{I}^{\otimes 2}\;+\;\frac{p}{d}\,|e\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle e|,\qquad|e\rangle\!\rangle=\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\,|a,a\rangle/\sqrt{d},

(59)

where
|e\rangle\!\rangle
is the normalised maximally entangled state on the
doubled single-qudit space.

depolarising_choi(d, p)

Returns the
d^{2}\!\times\!d^{2}
Choi matrix
of Eq. (
59
). Plugs directly into

noisy_brickwork_rtn_average(B, d, N, t, bd, channels)
as
one entry of the per-replica
channels
list of length
k
.

3.2
Incorporating noise into the replica tensor network

The noise channel is local and deterministic: in the
k
-replica
formalism, the action of
\mathcal{N}_{p}
on
k
copies of a single
qudit factorises as the per-site tensor product

\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{N}_{k}
, where potentially we can have different noise acting on different copies of the system. We will consider a circuit where noise acts locally on each qudit that was evolved with a unitary gate. In other words, the action of each unitary gate
U_{i,i+1}AU^{\dagger}_{i,i+1}
is replaced with
(\mathcal{N}_{i}\circ\mathcal{N}_{i+1})(U_{i,i+1}AU^{\dagger}_{i,i+1})
.

We now detail the derivation of the noisy transfer matrix in the way that actually
gets implemented in the code, see e.g.
[
50
,
49
]
.

Applying noise channel on qudits after the unitary gates leads to the replacement

\mathcal{T}_{i,i+1}\to\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}_{i}\right)\mathcal{T}_{i,i+1}=\sum_{\sigma,\pi}\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,\sigma}\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}_{i}\right)|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle\pi|\;.

(60)

When contracting with the next gate, the action of
\mathcal{N}^{\otimes k}
leads to the
noisy Gram matrix

[\tilde{G}(q;\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k})]_{\pi,\sigma}:=\langle\!\langle\pi\bigl|\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{N}_{k}\bigr|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle,

(61)

where
\mathcal{N}_{a}
is the Choi superoperator of the channel on
replica
a
(a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
matrix) and the inner product is over
the single-site
d^{2k}
-dim doubled replica space. For
identity

channels,
\tilde{G}
reduces to the ordinary Gram matrix

G_{\pi,\sigma}(d)=d^{\#(\pi^{-1}\sigma)}
, so

\tilde{G}
is the precise minimal generalization that captures the
noise.

The power of this formulation is that
changing the ensemble or
the noise model changes only the entries of
\tilde{G}
: the bulk
structure of the tensor network, how layers chain together, how the
boundaries contract, is untouched. Setting
\mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathbb{I}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}=\mathcal{N}
for
k=2
yields the

XEB
structure (one replica clean representing the classical computation, one noisy accounting for the quantum computation); setting

\mathcal{N}_{a}=\mathcal{N}
for every replica yields the symmetric

k
-copy noisy collision probability.

In essence, to assemble
\widetilde{G}
one taks the
d^{2k}
-dim
Kronecker product of the
k
Choi matrices and contract against the
permutation kets.
This strategy works even when the circuit is partially random and partially deterministic: one relevant example is a circuit with local random on-site Haar gates alternated with deterministic ones
[
67
,
68
]
.

In summary, the noisy contraction differs from the clean
Algorithm
A.1
by simply changing who are the building gates
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
that now incorporate the noisy Gram matrices.

noisy_brickwork_rtn_average(B, d, N, t, bd, channels)

Same as
brickwork_rtn_average
but with the inclusion of the noise acting on
each qudit. The per-replica Choi matrices live in

channels
(length
k
); for the depolarising case used in this
section, every entry is built with
depolarising_choi(d, p)
.

3.3
Worked example: relative entropy of coherence under symmetric noise

Coherence of a quantum state
\rho
relative to a fixed reference
basis (the computational basis
\{|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\}
throughout these notes)
is the property of having non-zero off-diagonal entries in that
basis. When the system is mixed, a standard quantitative diagnostic, cf. e.g.
Refs.
[
69
,
70
,
71
,
48
,
72
,
73
]
,
is the
relative entropy of coherence

C^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}(\rho)\;=\;\log\,\frac{\Tr(\rho^{2})}{\Tr(\rho^{2}_{\mathrm{diag}})}\;=\;S^{(2)}(\rho_{\mathrm{diag}})\,-\,S^{(2)}(\rho),

(62)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{diag}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\langle\boldsymbol{x}|\rho|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\,|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}|

is the fully dephased image of
\rho
in the chosen basis, and

S^{(2)}(\sigma)=-\log\Tr(\sigma^{2})
is the Rényi-2
entropy.
C^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}
vanishes when
\rho
is already
diagonal in
\{|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\}
(no off-diagonal weight, no coherence) and
is bounded above by
\log D
(saturated when
\rho
is pure and maximally delocalized). It
is the simplest non-linear diagnostic that is sensitive to the

quantumness
of the output bit-string distribution and not just
to its flatness.

For pure states evolving unitarily,
\Tr(\rho^{2})=1
and so

C^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}(|{}_{t}\rangle)=-\log\mathcal{I}^{(2)}(|{}_{t}\rangle)

is just the collision probability (up to a change in logarithmic base).
After one single layer, the system develops extensive coherences due to the unitary gates.
The noise starts to kick in and increases the thermal entropy, making the relative entropy of coherences to decrease to zero as
\rho_{t}\to\mathbb{I}/D
.

Figure 3:
Noisy random Haar-unitary brickwork on qubits, at every
integer depth and for
N\in\{8,16,32,64\}
. Panels (a) and
(c) plot the negative log purity
-\log\mathbb{E}[\Tr\,\rho_{t}^{2}]
at
p=0.10
and
p=0.20
respectively;
panels (b) and (d) plot the relative entropy of
coherence
\tilde{C}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}(\rho_{t})

at the same two noise rates. The coherence rises linearly with
depth, peaks at the sampling transition, and decays to zero as

\rho_{t}\to\mathbb{I}/D
becomes classical.

Both
\mathbb{E}[\Tr(\rho_{t}^{2})]
and

\mathbb{E}[\Tr(\rho_{t,\mathrm{diag}}^{2})]
are

k=2
doubled-replica observables. Crucially, they share the

same
noisy bulk transfer matrix, and
differ only by the top boundary:

\tilde{C}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}(\rho_{t})\;:=\;\log\,\frac{\langle\!\langle{}^{\mathrm{full}}_{2}\,|\,(\Phi_{t}^{(2)})_{\mathrm{noisy}}\,|\,\rho_{0}^{\otimes 2}\rangle\!\rangle}{\langle\!\langle{}^{\textup{IPR}}_{2}\,|\,(\Phi_{t}^{(2)})_{\mathrm{noisy}}\,|\,\rho_{0}^{\otimes 2}\rangle\!\rangle},

(63)

with
|{}^{\textup{IPR}}_{2}\rangle\!\rangle
the IPR boundary
of Eq. (
19
) (
b_{i}(\sigma)=d
on every site) and

|{}^{\mathrm{full}}_{2}\rangle\!\rangle=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}|s\rangle\!\rangle_{i}
the
full-chain swap boundary (the LP boundary of (
20
)
specialised to
A=\{1,\dots,N\}
, hence the swap on every site). The two contractions are produced by a single
call to the function
noisy_brickwork_rtn_average
with two different
boundary objects:
IPRBoundary(B, d)
for the denominator and

RenyiPurityBoundary(B, d, 1:N)
for the numerator.

Fig.
3
shows the noisy purity

\widetilde{S}_{(2)}^{\mathrm{PUR}}(t):=-\log\mathbb{E}[\Tr\,\rho_{t}^{2}]
and the relative entropy of
coherence
\tilde{C}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{rel}}(\rho_{t})
on
N\in\{8,16,32,64\}
qubits at two values of the local depolarising rate,

p=0.10
and
p=0.20
.
After the initial peak, the coherent evolution decays back to zero as the noisy steady state

\rho_{\infty}=\mathbb{I}_{D}/D
takes over with time.

3.4
Worked example: coherent information under symmetric noise

A second physically meaningful observable accessible to the noisy RTN
is the
coherent information
of the noisy encoding
channel
[
50
]
, the natural
error-resilient diagnostic
[
36
,
35
]
for the random circuits we are
discussing. We restrict ourselves to local depolarising noise.

The construction takes
K
logical qudits and entangles each with one
qudit of a clean reference system
R
of
K
qudits via
K
Bell
pairs. The remaining
N-K
qudits of the physical system
B
are
prepared in
|0\rangle^{\otimes(N-K)}
, and the noisy brickwork
acts on the entire
B
(the reference
R
is left untouched). In
symbols, the initial state is

\rho_{RB}(0)\;=\;|{}_{+}\rangle\langle{}_{+}|_{R,B_{1:K}}\;\otimes\;\bigl(|0\rangle\langle 0|\bigr)^{\otimes(N-K)}_{B_{K+1:N}},\qquad|{}_{+}\rangle=d^{-K/2}\,\sum_{\mathbf{j}}\,|\mathbf{j}\rangle_{R}\,|\mathbf{j}\rangle_{B},

(64)

and the encoded state at depth
t
is

\rho_{RB}(t)=(I_{R}\circ\mathcal{U}_{t})[\rho_{RB}(0)]
, where
I_{R}
is the identity channel on the reference, and
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is the noisy random circuit.
We assume the noise acts
within
the encoding circuit, i.e. a
single-qudit depolarising channel
\mathcal{N}_{p}
is applied to every
site of
B
after every unitary gate layer. Diagrammatically, with
time running upward,

\rho_{RB}(t)\;=\;\hbox to209.55pt{\vbox to139.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.60684pt\lower-45.78229pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(65)

The natural figure of merit is the
coherent information

I_{c}(\mathcal{U})=\mathrm{S}_{(2)}(\rho_{B}(t))-\mathrm{S}_{(2)}(\rho_{RB}(t))

[
74
,
75
,
76
]
, normalised by
K
:

I_{c}/K=1
signals perfect decodability,
I_{c}/K<1
irreversible
loss of logical information.
We consider the annealed averaged circuit

I_{c}^{(2)}(\mathcal{U})\;=\;\widetilde{S}_{(2)}^{\mathrm{PUR}}(B)-\widetilde{S}_{(2)}^{\mathrm{PUR}}(R\cup B)

(66)

which lifts to a
k=2
replica observable with the same noisy bulk
for both terms; only the top boundary changes.

Fig.
4
reports
I_{c}^{(2)}/K
as a function of
depth for
d=2
,
K=1
,
N\in\{8,16,32,64\}
at two
depolarising rates
p=0.05
(panel a) and
p=0.10

(panel b). The curves display the error-correction transition
cleanly: at fixed
p
they collapse onto a sharp crossover at a
critical depth
t_{c}(p)
, separating a low-depth regime where
the logical qudit is still recoverable (
I_{c}^{(2)}/K\approx 1
)
from a high-depth regime where decoherence has destroyed the logical
information (
I_{c}^{(2)}/K\approx-1
).

Figure 4:
Rényi-2 coherent information

I_{c}^{(2)}(R\!:\!B)/(K\,\log d)
of the noisy Haar-random encoder on

N_{B}
qubits as a
function of circuit depth
t
, for two values of the single-qubit
depolarising strength
p\in\{0.05,0.10\}
at fixed

K=1
reference qubit. RTN prediction at

N_{B}\in\{8,16,32\}
. As
N_{B}
grows the curves approach
a sharp crossover at a critical depth
t_{c}(p)
, the
error-correction transition between the recoverable phase

I_{c}^{(2)}\to+1
and the decohered phase
I_{c}^{(2)}\to-1
.

3.5
Worked example: the linear cross-entropy benchmark with asymmetric noise

The relative entropy of coherence diagnostic of
§
3.3
placed the same noise on both
replicas. In experiments, however, one almost always has access to
two
distinct
ensembles of states: the actual outputs of a
quantum processor running the noisy circuit, and the perfect outputs
of the same circuit simulated on a classical computer. The
asymmetric
k=2
replica observable that compares them is the

linear cross-entropy benchmark

\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}(t)\;=\;d^{N}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{N}}p_{\mathrm{cl}}(\boldsymbol{x})\,p_{\mathrm{n}}(\boldsymbol{x})\right]{\;-\;1},

(67)

where
p_{\mathrm{cl}}(\boldsymbol{x})=|\langle\boldsymbol{x}|U|{}_{0}\rangle|^{2}
is the
ideal output distribution, whereas the probability distribution

p_{\mathrm{n}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\langle\boldsymbol{x}|\mathcal{U}_{t}(|{}_{0}\rangle\langle{}_{0}|)|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle
is the
output from the noisy device. The observable
\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}
plays a central
role in benchmarking quantum-advantage regimes in current quantum devices, cf. Ref.
[
2
,
77
]
. The
-1
subtraction in Eq. (
67
) is the
standard normalisation that turns
\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}
into a fidelity-like
diagnostic: a perfect quantum simulation (
p_{\mathrm{n}}=p_{\mathrm{cl}}
, no
device error) gives
\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}\to 1
at depths
t\gtrsim\log N

where the Haar limit
d^{N}\,\mathbb{E}[\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}p^{2}]=2d^{N}/(d^{N}+1)
saturates,
while uniformly-random outputs (
p_{\mathrm{n}}\equiv d^{-N}
, fully decohered
device) give
\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}\to 0
. Empirically,
\chi_{\mathrm{XEB}}(t)
is
thus an exponentially-decaying proxy for the running fidelity of the
noisy circuit relative to its ideal classical simulation.

Exercise 3.5
.

Finite-rate error-correction transition.

Fig.
4
uses
K=1
logical qubit, which gives a
smooth crossover in
t
but no
sharp
finite-size effect. Following Ref.
[
78
]
, lift the construction to a finite encoding rate
r=K/N_{B}
held constant
as
N_{B}
grows, i.e.
K=r\,N_{B}
Bell pairs between
R
and the first

r\,N_{B}
sites of
B
. At fixed
r>0
the curves
I_{c}^{(2)}/k
at
different
(N_{B},t)
collapse onto a universal function of the
cumulative-noise parameter
f_{2}\propto p\,t
and develop a sharp
critical point at
f_{2}^{\rm crit}=(1-r)(1+g_{s,e})
, where

g_{s,e}=\log\tilde{G}_{s,e}(d,\mathcal{N})
encodes the
depolarising channel.
(a) Build the initial state, final state and bulk circuit for the RTN at a given input
K
.
(b) For
r=1/4
and a small grid of

(N_{B},p)
, sweep
t
and record
I_{c}^{(2)}/k
.
(c) Obtain the function
F=\mathbb{E}_{U}\!\left[\mathrm{tr}\!\left[\mathcal{U}_{t}\!\bigl(\ket{0}\!\bra{0}\bigr)\,U\ket{0}\!\bra{0}U^{\dagger}\right]\right]
with the appropriate RTN boundary state, and compute
f_{2}:=-\tfrac{2}{N}\log\tilde{F}
with
\tilde{F}=F-d^{-N}
.
(d) Reproduce the phase transition diagram plotting
I_{c}/K
against
f_{2}
.

4
Random circuits beyond Haar unitary

The statistical mechanics mapping of §§
2.2
–
2.4
extends naturally to other gate ensembles. The key abstraction is the
commutant
: given an ensemble
\mathcal{E}
of
q\times q
unitaries, the operators that commute with
k
pairs
U\otimes U^{*}
for every
U\sim\mathcal{E}
form a finite-dimensional algebra
\mathrm{Comm}_{k}(\mathcal{E})
. Picking a basis
\{|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\mathcal{E})\}
of this commutant and lifting it to the doubled
k
-replica space, we recover the same RTN construction with
\mathrm{S}_{k}
-spins replaced by spins of dimension
q_{\mathrm{eff}}=|\mathrm{Comm}_{k}(\mathcal{E})|
, the
\mathrm{S}_{k}
Gram matrix of Eq. (
12
) replaced by the analogous
\mathcal{E}
-Gram

G^{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma,\tau}(q)\;=\;\langle\!\langle\sigma|\tau\rangle\!\rangle\bigr|_{\dim=q},\qquad\mathrm{Wg}^{\mathcal{E}}(q)=\bigl(G^{\mathcal{E}}(q)\bigr)^{+},

(68)

and the Haar moment formula Eq. (
22
) replaced by the universal expansion

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{E}}\bigl[(U\otimes U^{*})^{\otimes k}\bigr]\;=\;\sum_{\sigma,\pi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\mathcal{E})}\mathrm{Wg}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\sigma,\pi}(d^{2})\,|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle\pi|.

(69)

The rest of the machinery, dressed transfer matrix, MPS evolution, boundary contraction, is identical to the unitary-Haar case. Implementing a new ensemble therefore reduces to (i) identifying the commutant basis
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\mathcal{E})
and (ii) writing the closed-form
\langle\!\langle\sigma|\tau\rangle\!\rangle
in terms of its combinatorial data; the companion package abstracts both behind a single
CommutantBasis
interface. In the rest of this section we work out two concrete cases: the orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(q)
(§
4.1
) and the Clifford group on qutrits (§
4.2
).

A note on notation.
In what follows we
keep the symbol
|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle
for any element of the commutant
basis
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\mathcal{E})
, even when
\sigma
is no longer a
permutation: for the orthogonal group
|\sigma\rangle\!\rangle
stands for a
Brauer diagram (perfect matching), while for the Clifford ensemble it stands
for a stochastic Lagrangian subspace. This abuse of notation is
deliberate, it lets the same MPS-contraction code, with the same
boundary kets, run unchanged on every ensemble.

4.1
Orthogonal Haar circuits

For two-qudit gates drawn from the orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(d^{2})
instead of
\mathrm{U}(d^{2})
, the commutant of
\mathrm{O}(q)
acting diagonally on
(\mathbb{C}^{q})^{\otimes k}
is the
Brauer algebra

B_{k}(q)

[
22
]
. Its natural basis consists of the perfect matchings of
2k
labelled points:
k
“upper” (ket) and
k
“lower” (bra) vertices, paired in any way that produces a fixed-point-free involution. In addition to the propagating strands, which reproduce the symmetric group
\mathrm{S}_{k}\subset B_{k}
, generic Brauer diagrams contain
caps
(two upper vertices paired) and
cups
(two lower vertices paired); the total number is
|B_{k}|=(2k-1)!!=1,3,15,105,945,\ldots
for
k=1,2,3,4,5
. The Brauer Gram matrix is given by the loop-counting formula

G^{\mathrm{O}}_{m,m^{\prime}}(q)\;=\;q^{L(m,m^{\prime})},

(70)

where
L(m,m^{\prime})
is the number of disjoint cycles produced by stacking
m
on top of
m^{\prime}
and identifying the middle vertices.

Figure 6:
(a)
\tilde{S}^{(2)}_{O}:=-\log\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{t}]
for an orthogonal-Haar brickwork on qubits, plotted as a function of circuit depth
t
for
N\in\{8,16,32,64\}
. The horizontal asymptote is the orthogonal stationary value Eq. (
71
). (b) Difference
\Delta\tilde{S}^{(2)}_{O}(t)=\tilde{S}^{(2)}_{O}(\infty)-\tilde{S}^{(2)}_{O}(t)
is plotted for different system sizes, highlighting saturation at fixed tolerance
\varepsilon
in a timescale that is logarithmic in system size
t_{\mathrm{AC}}\simeq\log(N)
.

At
k=2
, the Brauer algebra has three elements

|B_{2}|=(2\cdot 2-1)!!=3
: the identity
\iota
, the swap
s
already familiar from the symmetric group, and one additional element, the
crossing
diagram, which we denote
|c\rangle\!\rangle
. Concretely,
|c\rangle\!\rangle=\sum_{a,b}|a,a,b,b\rangle\!\rangle
glues the two upper vertices into a cap and the two lower vertices into a cup, producing the contraction
\sum_{a}|a,a\rangle\langle a,a|
that is invariant under
\mathrm{O}(q)
but not under
\mathrm{U}(q)
. The IPR boundary
|{}^{\textup{IPR}}_{2}\rangle\!\rangle
of Eq. (
19
) is unchanged (the computational-basis constraint does not see the choice of ensemble), and its overlap with
|c\rangle\!\rangle
also gives
d
. The orthogonal IPR therefore admits the closed-form stationary value (single global gate)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{O}}\bigl[\mathcal{I}^{(2)}\bigr]\;=\;\frac{3}{D+2},\qquad D=d^{N},

(71)

where the numerator
3=(2\cdot 2-1)!!=3!!
is the orthogonal-Haar
double factorial. The general
k
th moment on
\mathrm{O}(D)
reads

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{O}}[\mathcal{I}^{(k)}]=(2k-1)!!/[(D+2)(D+4)\cdots(D+2k-2)]
,
and for
k=2
it differs from the unitary value
2/(D+1)
by the
extra “crossing” diagrams of
\mathrm{O}(D)

[
45
]
. Plugging the Brauer Gram and Weingarten into the same dressed transfer matrix as the unitary case yields the local IPR dynamics; in code, switching from unitary to orthogonal is the one-liner

⬇

B_O

=

rtn
.
BrauerBasis
(2)

#

|B_O|

=

3

(orthogonal,

k

=

2)

val

=

rtn
.
brickwork_average
(
B_O
,

d
,

N
,

t
,

rtn
.
IPRBoundary
(
B_O
,

d
))

The corresponding
\tilde{S}^{(2)}_{O}:=-\log\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{t}]
is plotted in Fig.
6
(a). As for the unitary case, the saturation at a fixed precision to the orthogonal Haar value Eq. (
71
) occurs in a timescale that is logarithmic in system size.

Exercise 4.6
.

OTOC for orthogonal-Haar circuits.

The OTOC carries over verbatim to orthogonal-Haar: same boundary recipe, same MPS sweep, only the commutant changes. At
k=2
the Brauer algebra has
|\mathcal{B}_{2}|=3
basis elements (id, swap, and the crossing) and the OTOC profile is the column
G^{O}(s,\cdot;d)
of the
orthogonal
Gram Eq. (
70
).
(a) Compute the stationary (orthogonal Haar) value for
A_{1}=A_{2}=B_{1}=B_{2}=Z_{1}
.
(b) Implement the dynamics of the OTOC for a brickwork orthogonal circuit. At what timescale saturates to the Haar value? How does it behave compared to the unitary case?

4.2
Clifford random circuits

When the two-qudit gates are drawn uniformly from the Clifford group
\mathcal{C}_{N}
rather than from the full unitary group, the commutant for general
k
is generated by the
stochastic Lagrangian subspaces
of
\mathbb{F}_{d}^{2k}

[
24
]
. At
k=2
the Clifford ensemble is a unitary
2
-design for every
d
, so the Clifford and Haar moments coincide and Eq. (
69
) reduces to the standard
\mathrm{S}_{2}
formula.
For
d>2
, the first non-trivial case is at
k=3
. To make the discussion concrete we take qutrits (
d=3
), where one already feels the difference between the Clifford and unitary moments while the bookkeeping stays small.

For qutrits at
k=3
, the Clifford commutant has
|\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\mathrm{Cl}}|=8
basis elements, two more than the symmetric-group commutant
|\mathrm{S}_{3}|=6
. For the
i
th replica qudit, the two extra elements are the state

\begin{split}|Q_{3}()\rangle\!\rangle_{i}\;&=\;\left(\frac{1}{d}\,\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{d}}\,(I\otimes P)^{\otimes 3}\right)|\iota\rangle\!\rangle_{i},\\
|Q_{3}(12)\rangle\!\rangle_{i}\;&=\;\left(\frac{1}{d}\,\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{d}}\,(I\otimes P)^{\otimes 3}\right)|s\rangle\!\rangle_{i},\end{split}

(72)

where
s=(12)
is the swap acting on replicas
1
and
2
, and
\mathcal{P}_{d}=\{P_{a}\}_{a=1}^{d^{2}}
is an orthonormal Pauli basis on a single qutrit.
The total replica states are given by
|Q_{3}\sigma\rangle\!\rangle=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}|Q_{3}\sigma\rangle\!\rangle
for
\sigma=(),(12)
.
Plugging the eight-element commutant into Eq. (
69
) and contracting with the IPR boundary gives the closed-form Clifford stationary value of the third IPR. Eq. (20) of
[
28
]
(see also
[
56
]
) writes it as the ratio of two
q
-Pochhammer symbols,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Cl}}\bigl[\mathcal{I}^{(k)}\bigr]\;=\;\frac{(-d^{\,2-k};\,d)_{N}}{(-d;\,d)_{N}},\qquad(a;\,\xi)_{N}\;=\;\prod_{m=0}^{N-1}\bigl(1-a\,\xi^{m}\bigr),

(73)

which on qutrits at
k=3
gives
I_{\mathcal{C}}^{(3)}:=8/[(D+1)(D+3)]
.
To study the IPR dynamics, in code the only change with respect to the unitary brickwork is to swap the commutant basis,

⬇

B_Cl

=

rtn
.
CliffordBasis
(3,

3)

#

|B_Cl|

=

8

(Clifford,

qutrits,

k

=

3)

val

=

rtn
.
brickwork_average
(
B_Cl
,

3,

N
,

t
,

rtn
.
IPRBoundary
(
B_Cl
,

3))

which runs the same MPS sweep as in the unitary case on the larger commutant. Fig.
7
reports the dynamics of
\tilde{S}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{Cl}}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{(3)}]
on qutrits for the Clifford ensemble: panel (a) shows the bare RTN data converging to the dotted Clifford plateau, cf. Eq. (
73
), while panel (b) plots
\Delta\tilde{S}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{Cl}}
the difference between the stationary state and the time-evolving one.

Figure 7:
(a)
\tilde{S}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{Cl}}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{(3)}]
on qutrits for a Clifford-random brickwork (markers). (b) The difference
\Delta\tilde{S}^{(3)}_{\mathrm{Cl}}(t)
is plotted for various system sizes.

5
Discussion

The replica tensor network reduces the calculation of any
one-dimensional circuit-averaged polynomial of degree
k
in the gates to
the contraction of a two-dimensional statistical-mechanics model on the
commutant of the underlying gate group. The numerical cost is governed
by three parameters only — the replica count
k
, the local qudit
dimension
d
, and the effective MPS bond dimension
O(|\mathcal{B}|^{2})

— and is essentially independent of
d^{N}
. This makes the RTN a
practical device for hundreds of qudits, well beyond the reach of direct
state-vector simulation, while preserving analytical control through the
commutant-basis representation.

For simplicity we covered only the simplest scenarios, yet still tackled
physically motivated quantities: inverse participation ratios and
participation entropies, and measures of error-resilience in many-body
systems. The strength of the method is rooted in its versatility:
changing the gate, or adding noise or deterministic injections, only
modifies the bulk tensor via generalised Weingarten calculus; changing
observables or initial states only modifies the replica boundary
conditions.

Several directions not covered in these notes are immediate extensions
of the same machinery. On the geometric side, while we focused on
brickwork circuits, alternative geometries adapt naturally: random
matrix product states and random tensor networks are two such
classes
[
44
]
, as is the gluing of quantum
circuits
[
63
,
28
]
.

When the local Hilbert-space dimension is sufficiently large, a further simplification can be obtained using free-probability methods
[
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
]
. This reduces the number of internal degrees of freedom from
|\mathrm{S}_{k}|
to the Catalan number
C_{k}
, providing a complementary route to the irreducible-representation reduction for qubits discussed in Sec.
2.5
.

On the symmetry side, enforcing a global
U(1)

conservation
[
32
,
90
,
48
]
,
or more general finite-group symmetries, refines the commutant basis
into charge-resolved blocks. Other ensembles, such as fermionic
circuits, can be handled by the same machinery, building on recent
advances in the matchgate
commutant
[
91
,
92
,
93
]
.
Even measurements can be added, provided additional care is taken on the replica extrapolations, cf. Ref.
[
94
,
95
,
96
]

Each of these extensions reuses the same two abstractions, a commutant-basis spec and a boundary ket, that we have introduced here, and we hope that the present notes serve as a stepping stone for the reader to take them up.

and Emmy Noether Programme proposal “Digital Quantum Matter

Code and Data Availability.

The companion package
ReplicaTN
(v0.3.0) is
released under the MIT license at

https://github.com/xturkesh/ReplicaTN
, to appear in Zenodo
[
97
]
in a future version with the data.

Appendix A
Numerical implementation walk-through

A.1
Introduction to the numerical implementation

We established that the circuit-averaged quantities can be computed as the contraction of a two-dimensional tensor network with
k!
-state effective spins.
While I encourage the reader to reproduce with their own code the ideas discussed here, e.g. supported by open libraries such as
ITensor
or
TeNPy

[
98
,
61
]
, I attach with the notes a minimalistic C++ codebase with Python frontend via
pybind11
that build the Gram matrix, the averaged gate tensor, and the brickwork MPS contraction directly from the formulae of Sec.
2
.
Every call we use below is one of
SymmetricBasis
,
gram_matrix
,
IPRBoundary
,
brickwork_rtn_average
(and their noisy/orthogonal/Clifford analogues).

The full workflow is summarised in Algorithm
A.1
. The strategy it encodes is:

1.

Build the local tensors: the Gram matrix, the Weingarten matrix, and the replica transfer matrix.

2.

Encode the initial state and boundary conditions as an MPS.

3.

Evolve the MPS through each layer of the replica tensor network using the transfer matrix as an MPO.

4.

Contract with the final boundary to extract the result.

We focus on the collision probability (
k=2
) for concreteness, where the effective spin dimension is
q_{\mathrm{eff}}=2!=2
, but the code generalises to arbitrary
k
and other replica boundary conditions.

Algorithm 1. Replica tensor-network contraction of a brickwork circuit average.

commutant basis
\mathcal{B}_{k}
of size
n_{\mathcal{B}}
; qudit dimension
d
; chain length
N
; depth
t
; boundary vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathcal{B}}}
(or site-dependent profile) encoding the observable.

G\leftarrow

n_{\mathcal{B}}\times n_{\mathcal{B}}
Gram matrix,
G_{\sigma,\tau}=\langle\!\langle\sigma|\tau\rangle\!\rangle(d)

G_{(2)}\leftarrow
Gram matrix at
q=d^{2}

\mathrm{Wg}_{(2)}\leftarrow G_{(2)}^{-1}

\triangleright
pseudo-inverse when
d^{2}<k
, else the exact inverse

build the 4-index averaged gate
T[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]=\delta_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{Wg}_{(2),\sigma_{1},\pi}\,G_{\pi,\tau_{1}}\,G_{\pi,\tau_{2}}

set the per-site initial amplitude
c_{\mathrm{init}}(\sigma)=\sum_{\tau}\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma,\tau;d)

\triangleright
here for the product state
|0\rangle^{\otimes N}

build the product MPS
|{}_{0}\rangle=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}c_{\mathrm{init}}

for

\ell=1

to

t

do

\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\{(1,2),(3,4),\dots\}
if
\ell
odd, else
\{(2,3),(4,5),\dots\}

\triangleright
brickwork pattern

for

(i,i+1)\in\mathcal{P}

do

apply
T
on sites
(i,i+1)
, SVD-truncate to bond dim
\chi

end

for

end

for

\langle\Lambda\rangle\leftarrow\langle b^{\otimes N}|{}_{t}\rangle

\triangleright
inner product with the observable boundary

return

\langle\Lambda\rangle

A.2
Permutation basis, Gram and Weingarten matrices

The effective spins of the replica tensor network are elements of
the commutant basis returned by
SymmetricBasis(k)
(size

|\mathrm{S}_{k}|=k!
for the unitary case; orthogonal and Clifford analogues
swap in
BrauerBasis(k)
or
CliffordBasis(k, d)
).
The two algebraic ingredients of the contraction are the Gram matrix

G_{\sigma,\tau}(\chi)=\chi^{\#(\sigma^{-1}\tau)}
of
Eq. (
12
) and its Moore–Penrose pseudo-inverse

\mathrm{Wg}(\chi)=G(\chi)^{+}
of
§
2.3.2
, both tabulated at the two
dimensions that appear in the brickwork:
\chi=d
on a single site,
and
\chi=d^{2}
on a two-site averaged gate. In code,

⬇

import

numpy

as

np

=

2

#

qubits

k

=

2

#

collision

probability

B

=

rtn
.
SymmetricBasis
(
k
)

#

|B|

=

k!

for

unitary

G

=

rtn
.
gram_matrix
(
B
,

d
)

#

|B|

x

|B|

at

single-site

dim

d

G2

=

rtn
.
gram_matrix
(
B
,

d

*

d
)

#

at

two-site

dim

d^2

Wg2

=

rtn
.
weingarten_matrix
(
B
,

d

*

d
)

#

=

inv(G2)

assert

.

(

@

,

np
.
eye
(
len
(
B
)))

The pseudo-inverse
pinv
matters only when

\chi<k
, where
G(\chi)
has a non-trivial kernel because the
permutation states become linearly dependent in the doubled
single-site space (see
[
20
]
and
Appendix
); for the brickwork at
\chi=d^{2}

and
k\leq 3
the matrix is invertible and

returns
the exact inverse.

A.3
Building the transfer matrix as an MPO

The central object is the replica transfer matrix
\mathcal{T}^{(k)}_{i,i+1}
, which acts on two neighbouring replica spins. For
k=2
, this is a
4\times 4
matrix (input: two
q_{\mathrm{eff}}
-dimensional spins; output: two
q_{\mathrm{eff}}
-dimensional spins).

The dressed transfer matrix Eq. (
39
) is constructed as follows:

1.

Compute the two-site Weingarten matrix:
\mathrm{Wg}(d^{2})
.

2.

Compute the single-site Gram matrix:
G(d)
.

3.

The tensor element
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
couples input permutations
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
to output permutations
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
via

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}=\sum_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\mathrm{Wg}_{\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime},\pi\cdot\pi^{\prime}}(d^{2})\cdot G_{\pi,\tau_{1}}(d)\cdot G_{\pi^{\prime},\tau_{2}}(d),

(A.1)

where
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime}
denotes the combined two-site permutation.

⬇

print
(
T_gate
.
shape
)

#

(

|B|,

|B|,

|B|,

|B|

)

i.e.

(2,2,2,2)

for

k=2

Internally,
averaged_gate_tensor
builds the same four-index object as Eq. (
39
) from the Gram and Weingarten matrices already returned by
gram_matrix(B, d)
and by the fucntion
weingarten_matrix(B, d
ˆ
2)
. The result is a plain
|B|^{2}\times|B|^{2}

numpy
array that the brickwork driver below reshapes into a rank-4 tensor on demand.

For
k=2
and
d=2
, the effective local dimension is
|B|=2
, so the transfer tensor has
2^{4}=16
entries. For
k=3
(
|B|=6
) or
k=4
(
|B|=24
) the tensor grows to
36^{2}
and
576^{2}
entries, respectively (however, these tensors are very sparse and most entries are zeros). The brickwork contraction proceeds at MPS bond dimension
\chi\sim O(|B|^{2})
, which stays modest at the values of
k
we use in practice.

A.4
Setting up the MPS and evolving

We now describe the full pipeline for computing the average IPR via MPS evolution.

⬇

""
"Circuit-averaged

kth

IPR

of

a

depth-t

Haar

brickwork

on

N

qudits

of

dimension

d,

computed

via

the

high-level

ReplicaTN

driver."
""

import

replicatn

as

rtn

B

=

rtn
.
SymmetricBasis
(
k
)

bd

=

rtn
.
IPRBoundary
(
B
,

d
)

return

rtn
.
brickwork_average
(

,

,

,

,

,

=1
e
-13,

maxdim
=

The driver
brickwork_rtn_average
takes care of (i) building the boundary product MPS
init_amplitudes(B, d)
on every site, (ii) applying
averaged_gate_tensor(B, d)
on the odd / even bonds in turn, (iii) SVD-truncating to bond dimension
chi_max
, (iv) contracting against the per-site IPR boundary
b(\sigma)=d
. All four steps go through the minimal
ReplicaTN
tensor network library (
cpp/ReplicaTN.h
), cf. Ref.
[
98
,
61
]
for similar (and more versatile!) implementations. The Python frontend (
python/replicatn/
) wraps them via
pybind11
and adds the symbolic algebra (commutants, Weingarten, gates, boundaries) on top in
algebra.py
.

Convergence with bond dimension.
For
k=2
, the MPS bond dimension required for convergence is modest:
\chi\sim q_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}=4
is often sufficient. For
k=3
(
q_{\mathrm{eff}}=6
), one needs
\chi\sim 36
, and for
k=4
(
q_{\mathrm{eff}}=24
),
\chi\sim 576
. These values reflect the fact that the entanglement in the replica tensor network saturates at a level determined by the effective local dimension
[
51
]
.
